Preface 
This Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor of Rajasthan 
under Article 151 of the Constitution of India. 
This Report contains the results of Performance Audit of 'Public Health 
Infrastructure and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan' for the 
period 2016-22. This Performance Audit assesses the measures taken by State 
Government to ensure adequate availability of infrastructure and healthcare 
services, drugs, medicines, equipment and human resources to beneficiaries in 
the State. 
The Performance Audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing 
Standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India. 
V Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure vi 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Executive Summary 
Health is a vital indicator of human development and a basic ingredient of 
economic and social development. A robust public health infrastructure and 
effective management of health services are utmost important to ensure good 
health of citizens. 
This Performance Audit is an assessment of the healthcare services provided in 
the State of Rajasthan. It examined whether Government of Rajasthan took 
adequate measures to ensure adequate availability of infrastructure and 
healthcare services, drugs, medicines, equipment and human resources to 
beneficiaries. The Performance Audit covered the period 2016-22. 
Audit observed that there was shortage of Doctors, Specialists, Nurses and 
Paramedics in the Primary, Secondary and Tertiary Healthcare Institutions. The 
State Government should address the shortage of health care professionals in a 
time-bound manner by adopting a clearly defined recruitment strategy as well 
as ensuring their equitable distribution across the State. 
Audit noticed that essential minimum assured services like General Surgery, 
Paediatrics, Orthopaedics, and Accident and Trauma Wards were deficient in 
IPD of the Government Medical Institutions (GMis). Deficiencies were also 
noticed in services such as Emergency, Intensive Care Units, Blood Banks of 
GMis. The State Government may ensure availability of the required Minimum 
Assured Services, like General Surgery, Paediatrics, Orthopaedics, 
Emergency, Intensive Care Unit, Blood Banks, etc. as per IPHS in the GMis of 
the State. 
There was non-availability of adequate Radiology (in 12 DHs out of 34 DHs), 
Pathology (partially available in all 34 DHs ), Dietary (in 18 DHs out of 34 
DHs ), Ambulance (in Seven DHs out of 34 DHs) and Mortuary (in one DH out 
of 34 DHs) services as per IPHS in the test-checked GMis. The State 
Government should make efforts to provide adequate services such as 
Radiology, Pathology, Dietary, Ambulance and Mortuary Services as per IPHS 
in the GMis of the State. 
There was shortfall of availability of essential drugs in test-checked GMis 
and district drug warehouses in the State. The State Government may take 
necessary steps to ensure sufficient availability of essential drugs in the 
hospitals and drug warehouses. 
Audit observed that medicines issued to medical units were less than the 
quantity demanded and that there were delays in finalizing rate contracts. It 
was also observed that purchase orders were placed when the buffer stock was 
much less than the prescribed buffer level. The State Government should 
specify reorder point at which purchase orders are placed and put in place a 
mechanism of system generated purchase orders for maintenance of prescribed 
buffer stocks. 
Delays were noticed in receipt of quality test reports from the laboratories 
which delayed the issue of medicines by the warehouses to the medical units, 
vii Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Executive Summary 
contributing to non-availability of medicines at the medical units. The State 
Government may ensure timely testing of drug samples and receipt of quality 
test reports on time. 
It was found that test-checked GMis purchased drugs locally without quality 
test reports. The State Government may ensure issue of drugs to GMls only 
after receipt of quality test reports. 
Equipment required as per Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) in various 
categories were not available in the test-checked GMis. The State Government 
may ensure availability of essential equipment in the GMis as per IPHS, as 
well as their periodic maintenance and calibration. 
There was shortage of healthcare infrastructure across the desert, tribal and 
plain areas of the State. The State Government did not have a concrete plan to 
address the shortage and prioritise areas with shortfall for setting up of 
healthcare infrastructure. The State Government may ensure availability of 
healthcare institutions as per Indian Public Health Standards in the State and 
their equitable distribution to make healthcare accessible for all. 
Deficiencies were noticed in the provision of basic amenities like separate 
toilet facilities for male and female, electricity, and potable drinking water in 
the Government Medical Institutions (GMis) of the State. The State 
Government should ensure availability of basic amenities like separate toilet 
facilities, electricity and potable drinking water in all the Government Medical 
Institutions of the State. 
Capital Expenditure constituted only 6.67 per cent of the total expenditure on 
healthcare. The State Government may make efforts to augment the capital 
expenditure in healthcare sector. 
The percentage of out-of-pocket expenditure to total health expenditure 
decreased in Rajasthan during the period 2016-20. However, it constituted 
47.40 per cent of total healthcare expenditure during 2019-20, indicating low 
financial protection available to households towards healthcare payments in 
the State. The State Government may take necessary steps to increase the 
budgetary allocation for healthcare sector both as a percentage of State 
Budget as well as Gross State Domestic Product, in line with the National 
Health Policy. 
There was non-availability of required tests, non-conduct of yoga sessions and 
inadequate screening of Non-Communicable Diseases in adults in the Health 
and Wellness Centres established under Ayushman Bharat scheme in the State. 
The State Government may ensure proper functioning of Health and Wellness 
Centres as envisaged in the guidelines of Ayushman Bharat-Health Wellness 
Centre. 
There were delays in issuing and renewing of drug manufacturing licenses and 
regular annual inspections were not carried out in any of the test-checked 
manufacturing units. The State Government should ensure that issue and 
Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure Vlll 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Executive Summary 
renewal of manufacturing licenses are done in a time-bound manner and 
annual inspections are conducted regularly. 
Action against firms manufacturing Not of Standard Quality drugs was 
significantly delayed. The State Government should fix responsibility of 
officers concerned for failure to ensure timely and appropriate action against 
the manufacturers of Not of Standard Quality Drugs. 
Audit observed that neither the composition nor the functioning of Rajasthan 
Medical Council was in accordance with the requirements of Rajasthan 
Medical Act and Pharmacy Inspectors had not been appointed in the State by 
State Pharmacy Council. The State Government should monitor the functioning 
of the State Medical Council and State Pharmacy Council to ensure effective 
regulation by these bodies. 
Rajasthan had not formulated any vision/ roadmap/ strategy for achievement of 
Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs ). The State Government may expedite 
the alignment of SDGs with the State Government Vision 2030.

1.1 Introduction 
Chapter-I 
Introduction 
Health is a vital indicator of human development and a basic ingredient of 
economic and social development. A robust public health infrastructure and 
effective management of health services are of vital importance to ensure good 
health of citizens. The public health infrastructure comprises of hospitals and 
other healthcare institutions while the health services comprise of services such 
as emergency, preventive, rehabilitative, long-term, hospital, diagnostic, 
primary, palliative, and home care. 
These services can be broadly divided into three categories, namely: 
(a) Line Services: Services directly related to patient care, like Out-Patient 
Department (OPD), In-Patient Department (IPD), Emergency, Super Specialty, 
Intensive Care Units, Operation Theatre, Blood Bank, Maternity and Diagnostic 
Services etc.; 
(b) Support Services: Services indirectly related to patient care, like Oxygen 
Services, Dietary Services, Laundry Services, Bio-Medical Waste 
Management, Ambulance Services and Mortuary Services etc.; and 
(c) Auxiliary Services: Services for facilitating the delivery of healthcare 
services, like patient safety facilities, patient registration, grievance/ complaint 
redressal and stores etc. 
1.2 Healthcare facilities in Rajasthan 
The landscape of public healthcare facilities in the State is structured into three 
levels for providing primary care, secondary care and tertiary care. Primary care 
and secondary care are provided by the Department of Medical, Health and 
Family Welfare (DMH&FW), Government of Rajasthan (GoR) while tertiary 
healthcare is provided by the Department of Medical Education (DME), GoR. 
The various healthcare institutions providing primary, secondary and tertiary 
care are shown in Chart 1.1 below:

The Government of Rajasthan (GoR) set up the Rajasthan Medical Services 
Corporation Limited (RMSCL ), a State Public Sector Undertaking, on 4 May 
2011 as a Centralised Procurement Agency for procuring generic medicines, 
surgical and suture items and medical equipments at the most economical cost 
for all the Government Medical Institutions under DMH&FW and DME. 
1.3 Public Health Funding 
The State Government makes budgetary provisions under the Annual Budget 
for the State's health sector. Financial assistance under the National Health 
Mission (NHM) is received from the Government of India (Gol) with 
corresponding share of the State Government in the ratio of 60:40. The State 
Health Society (SHS) disburses these funds to the District Health Societies 
(DHSs ), headed by the District Collector for further release to the Blocks, who 
in tum further disburse the funds to various implementing units (such as 
Community Health Centres, Primary Health Centres, Health Sub-Centres, etc.).

1.3.1 Expenditure on Healthcare 
Expenditure on health and family welfare is an important parameter to gauge 
the importance given to this sector by the Government. 
National Health Policy (NHP) proposes raising public health expenditure to 2.5 
per cent of the Gross Domestic Product (GDP) in a time-bound manner upto 
2025. Further, NHP also envisaged increasing State health sector spending to 
more than 8 per cent of the State budget by 2020. Funds expended during the 
period 2016-17 to 2021-22 on Health and Family Welfare by the GoR are given 
in Table 1.1 below:

Chapter-I: Introduction 
Status of Government Medical Colleges in the State 
Medical Education Department is the Administrative Department for the 
Government Medical and Dental Colleges situated in the State. Out of the 331 
districts in the State, there were eight Medical Colleges in seven districts upto 
31 March 2016. To augment tertiary healthcare in the State, seven new Medical 
Colleges were established in seven districts during 2016-22. Further, four 
Medical Colleges have been established in 2022-23 and construction work of 
12 more Medical Colleges in 12 districts is under progress. Details are given in 
Appendix 1.2.

1.6 Performance against Sustainable Development Goal-3 
The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 2030, also known as the Global 
Goals, were adopted by the United Nations in 2015 as a universal call for action 
to end poverty, protect the planet, and ensure that by 2030 all people enjoy peace 
and prosperity. The 17 SDGs and 169 targets are part of the 2030 Agenda for 
Sustainable Development adopted by 193 Member States at the UN General 
Assembly Summit in September 2015 and came into effect on 1 January 2016. 
The Planning Department (PD), GoR is the nodal department for the 
implementation of SDGs in the State. 
In Rajasthan there are 33 districts before Gazette Notification. Gazette Notification issued 
(5 August 2023) regarding constitution of 19 new districts Jaipur Urban, Jaipur Rural, 
Kekri, Sanchore, Khairthal-Tijara, Anupgarh, Gangapur City, Neem ka Thana, Phalodi, 
Dudu, Beawar, Didwana-Kuchaman, Salumber, Shahpura, Jodhpur Urban, Jodhpur Rural, 
Balotra. Out of 19 new districts, two districts (Jaipur and Jodhpur) were earlier included in 
33 districts. Hence, total 50 districts in Rajasthan.

1. 7 Consideration of Ayushman Bharat 
Ayushman Bharat (AB), the flagship health scheme of the Government of India, 
was launched in September 2018 to achieve Universal Health Coverage as 
recommended in the National Health Policy, 2017. AB adopts a continuum of care 
approach, comprising of two inter-related components, which are Health and 
Wellness Centres (HWCs) and Pradhan Mantri-Jan Arogya Yojana (PM-JAY). 
In August 2018, Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) was signed between 
National Health Authority (NHA) and GoR to implement the PM-JAY. After 13 
7 Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-I: Introduction 
months of signing the MoU, PM-JAY was introduced in Rajasthan on 1 September, 
2019 by integrating it with the ongoing State health scheme Bhamashah Swasthya 
Bima Yojana (BSBY) under a new name, Ayushman Bharat-Mahatma Gandhi 
Rajasthan Swasthya Bima Yojana (AB-MGRSBY). National Food Security Act 
beneficiaries as well as households fulfilling the criteria as per Socio Economic and 
Caste Census (SECC) 2011 database are eligible to avail benefits under AB­
MGRSBY. As on March 2022, in Rajasthan 59,71,496 families were identified as 
beneficiaries under SECC 2011. As on April 2022 1,27 ,29, 111 number of families 
were registered under Chiranjeevi Yojana which included PM-JAY families. 
1.8 Audit Objectives 
The Performance Audit was carried out to assess: 
1. Planning andfundsfor healthcare sector in the State; 
11. Quality of healthcare infrastructure in the State; 
111. Availability of quality drugs, medicines and equipment; 
1v. Availability of the human resources; 
v. Funding and expenditure of Central schemes in healthcare sector; 
v1. Regulatory mechanisms for ensuring quality healthcare services in the 
State; and 
vii. Improvement in the health and well-being of people as per SDG 3. 
1.9 Audit Criteria 
The Performance Audit was benchmarked against the criteria derived from the 
following sources: 
• National Health Policy, 2017; 
• Sustainable Development Goals; 
• Medical Council of India (replaced by National Medical Commission in 
2019) Act, 1956; 
• Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS), 2012; 
• Clinical Establishment Act, 201 O; 
• Drugs & Cosmetics Act, 1940; 
• Pharmacy Act, 1948; 
• Bio Medical Waste Management Rules, 2016; 
• Atomic Energy (Radiation Protection) Rules, 2004; 
• World Health Organization norms; 
• Assessor's Guidebook for Quality Assurance in Government Healthcare 
Centres published by MoH&FW in 2013 and 2014; 
• Rajasthan Transparency in Public Procurement Act, 2012 and Rules, 2013; 
• Manuals, Orders, Circulars and Scheme Guidelines issued by Gol and GoR. 
1.10 Audit Scope and Methodology 
An Entry Conference was conducted with the relevant Departments in October 
2021 wherein audit objectives, audit criteria, selection of units, audit 
methodology and scope of Performance Audit were discussed. 
The Performance Audit was conducted from October 2021 to June 2023 
covering the period 2016-17 to 2021-22 through test-check of records at 
DMH&FW, DME and Planning Department. Audit also test-checked records in 
the Offices of the Managing Director, Rajasthan Medical Services Corporation

and Management of Health Services in Rajas than"

Limited; Director (Public Health); Mission Director, National Health Mission; 
Government Testing Laboratory, Jaipur; Chief Engineer, National Health 
Mission and regulatory bodies, i.e. Drug Controller, Rajasthan Nursing Council, 
Rajasthan Medical Council, Rajasthan Pharmacy Council, Rajasthan Para­
Medical Council, Commissioner, Department of Medical Education and 
Director, Economics and Statistics, Planning Department. 
Eight districts2 (25 per cent) out of thirty-three districts in the State were 
selected through simple random sampling using iDEA Software pertaining to 
DMH&FW. In the eight selected districts, three Medical Colleges3, six attached

three Medical College Drug Warehouses were also selected. In the selected 
districts, Chief Medical and Health Officer, District Hospitals, Sub-District

Officer, Community Health Centres, Primary Health Centres, Health Wellness 
Centres and Health Sub-Centres were also selected.

views of GoR expressed in the Exit Conference have been appropriately 
incorporated in the Report.

Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 12 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-II 
Human Resources 
The Government of Rajas than did not have a long-term plan for recruitment and 
staffing in the healthcare sector. In the Primary and Secondary healthcare 
institutions, there was shortage of Doctors (35.51 per cent), Specialist Doctors 
(38.55 per cent), Nurses (18.56 per cent) and Paramedics (55.88 per cent), 
which was more pronounced in the desert and tribal areas of the State. 
Similarly, there was shortage of Doctors (21.45 per cent), Specialist Doctors 
(24.89 per cent), Nurses (21.02 per cent) and Paramedics (44.93 per cent) in 
Tertiary Care institutions also. The shortage of manpower was especially severe 
in case of Contractual Staff (50.19 per cent). 
2.1 Planning and Assessment of Human Resources 
The health workforce includes clinical staff such as physicians, nurses, 
pharmacists and dentists, as well as management and support staff, i.e. those 
who do not deliver services directly but are essential to the performance of 
health systems, such as managers, ambulance drivers and accountants. 
Audit observed that the Government of Rajasthan did not have a long-term plan 
for recruitment and staffing in the health sector. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that the recruitment is being done 
according to the budget announcements and directions received from the State 
Government from time to time. However, no reasons for not framing a long­
term plan for recruitment and staffing in the health sector has been furnished. 
2.2 Availability of Human Resources 
The delivery of quality healthcare services in hospitals largely depends on the 
adequate availability of doctors, staff nurses and para medical staff. 
The World Health Organization (WHO) recommended benchmark for doctor­
population ratio is 1: 1,000. The requirement of doctors in Rajasthan is 82,50611 
as per the norm recommended by WHO. As per the Indian Medical Register of 
National Medical Commission, a total of 49,242 doctors were registered upto 
2021 in Rajasthan. This shows that doctor-population ratio of Rajasthan is 1: 167 6 
11 Requirement of Doctors = Projected Population 8,25,06,000/1000, as 8,25,06,000 is the 
Projected Population ofRajasthan (as of July 2021) (Source: Progress Report of Medical 
Health and National Health Mission Rajasthan for 2021-22 issued by Department of 
Medical,Health & Family Welfare, Government ofRajasthan). 
13 Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-II : Human Resources 
against the WHO benchmark of 1: 1000. Thus, there was a 40 per cent shortage 
of doctors in the State. 
The State Government stated (May 2023) that the reasons for shortfall are newly 
created posts, voluntary retirement, superannuation, resignation and deaths. 
GoR had replied for shortfall in doctors in Government Sector only. Moreover, 
the reply is not tenable as the GoR did not prepare recruitment plan for filling 
up the vacancies of doctors. 
The reply is silent about shortage of doctors in Private Sector.

2.2.3 Vacancy position of doctors under DMH&FW 
The minimum essential and desirable requirements of human resources for 
primary and secondary level are prescribed in Indian Public Health Standards 
(IPHS) 2012. 
IPHS requires the services of at least one Medical Officer at a Primary Health 
Centre level. IPHS prescribes Specialty services from the secondary level 
hospital and minimum requirement of 32 to 68 doctors 16 and 20 to 24 doctors17 
at district level and sub district hospitals respectively depending on the bed 
strength of the hospital. In the case of Community Health Centres, the minimum 
requirement was 10 doctors. Status of availability of doctors in the test-checked 
Government Medical Institutions under Department of Medical, Health and 
Family Welfare are as shown in Table 2.6: 
14 In the 2,361 PHCs of the State as on 31 January 2023.

beds: 58 and 500 beds: 68). 
17 Minimum requirement of doctors at SDHs: (31-50 beds: 20 and 51-100 beds: 24).

Audit observed that: 
• In all 34 DHs against a requirement of 1,385 doctors as per IPHS, 1,532 
doctors were available and there was excess of 147 doctors. However, 
there was shortage of 539 doctors against sanctioned post of 2,071 
doctors. 
• In four test-checked SDHs against a requirement of 92 doctors as per 
IPHS, 36 doctors were available and there was shortage of 56 doctors. 
Further, there was shortage of 22 doctors against sanctioned post of 58 
doctors. 
• In eight test-checked CHCs against a requirement of 80 doctors as per 
IPHS, 50 doctors were available and there was shortage of 30 doctors. 
Further, there was shortage of 16 doctors against sanctioned post of 66 
doctors. 
• In test-checked 16 PHCs against a requirement of 16 doctors as per 
IPHS, 17 doctors were available and there was excess of one doctor. 
Further, there was shortage of two doctors against sanctioned post of 19 
doctors. 
This shows uneven distribution of manpower which further affect to medical 
facilities at grass root level.

2.2.9 Shortage of Manpower in Trauma Centers 
Department of Medical, Health and Family Welfare, GoR accorded approval for 
construction of 101 Trauma Centres up to March 2024. Out of 101 Trauma Centers, 
74 Trauma Centers were constructed and are functional. The remaining 27 Trauma 
Centres are also functioning in existing Government Medical Institutions buildings 
with available staff. 
Out of the eight test-checked District Hospitals, Trauma Centre/unit was not 
functional in 50 per cent of the District Hospitals23. These Trauma Centers were 
not functional due to lack of human resources. As a result, a high percentage of the 
patients in accident cases were being referred to the nearest operational Trauma 
Care Centers. Precious time was thus lost in transferring the patients, which in turn 
delayed their medical care. In the remaining operational Trauma Centers, there was 
a shortage of dedicated staff. 
The State Government stated (February 2023) that to meet the shortage of 
manpower in Trauma Centres, staff is being deployed through transfer from other 
hospitals. It was further stated (January 2024) that out of the sanctioned 498 posts 
of doctors/specialists, 294 posts are vacant in Trauma Centres.

sustainability of contractual human resources and planned for their inclusion in the 
State budget.

There was lack of sufficient registration facilities for OPD services at District 
Hospitals. Essential minimum assured services like General Surgery, Dental 
Care, Paediatrics, Burn Ward, ENT, Ophthalmology, Orthopaedics and 
Accident and Trauma Wards in IPD of the Government Medical Institutions 
were inadequate. 
Deficiencies were also noticed in services such as Emergency, Intensive Care 
Units and Blood Banks of Government Medical Institutions. The State was 
unable to achieve universal immunisation. 
While radiology services were found deficient, most of the Government 
Medical Institutions could not provide the full range of pathological 
investigations as required under IPHS. 
The State Government was unable to fully address the shortage of ambulances 
in the State. Shortfall was also witnessed in the provision of auxiliary services 
in the Government Medical Institutions including firefighting services. 
3.1 Delivery of Healthcare Services in Government Medical Institutions 
Delivery of services plays an important role in providing medical services to 
patients in the Government Medical Institutions (GMls). High-quality 
healthcare services involve the right care, at the right time and responding to 
the users' needs and preferences. Healthcare Services provided in GMis are 
mainly classified into three parts, viz. (a) Line services, (b) Support services 
and ( c) Auxiliary services. 
These services can be broadly divided into three categories namely: 
(a) Line Services: Services directly related to patient care, like Outdoor 
Patient Department (OPD), Indoor Patient Department (IPD), Emergency, 
Super Specialty, Intensive Care Units, Blood Bank, Maternity and 
Diagnostic Services etc.; 
(b) Support Services: Services indirectly related to patient care, like Oxygen 
Services, Dietary Services, Laundry Services, Ambulance Services and 
Mortuary Services etc.; 
(c) Auxiliary Services: Services for facilitating the delivery of healthcare 
services, like patient safety facilities, patient registration, grievance/ 
complaint redressal and stores.

3.1.1 Line services 
Line services in a hospital are directly related to timely and quality healthcare 
of patients. 
Out-Patient Department Services 
Out-Patient Department (OPD) is a part of the hospital with allotted physical 
facilities and medical and other staff, with regularly scheduled hours, to 
provide care for visiting patients who are not admitted. Details of availability 
of OPD services are shown in Appendix 3.1 (i).

(iii) Productivity of /PD services at State and District Hospitals 
Bed Occupancy Rate (BOR) 
The productivity of IPD services can be evaluated through Bed Occupancy 
Rate (BOR31) which reflects the productivity in the use of available hospital 
beds and is an indicator of quality of services, infrastructure, trained staff, 
patient care, and satisfaction provided by the facility. As per IPHS, it is 
expected that the BOR of a hospital should be at least 80 per cent. 
As per NITI Aayog's report on 'Best Practices in the Performance of District 
Hospitals', a high BOR is an indicator of health system under pressure. The 
report states that hospitals cannot operate at 100 per cent occupancy, as spare 
bed capacity is needed to accommodate variations in demand. Lack of 
available beds increases delays in emergency departments, causes patients to 
be placed on clinically inappropriate wards and increases the rate of hospital­
acquired infections. This also puts staff under pressure to free up beds that can 
pose a risk to patient safety. 
Similarly, the report also states that very low BOR (<42 per cent) at primary 
healthcare level indicates lack of medically trained personnel, irregular supply 
of drugs and other medical supplies and a complete breakdown in the transfer 
and referral system. 
Audit analysed the information of BOR of 91 hospitals32 in the State for the 
year 2022 and noticed that: 
31 Bed Occupancy Rate = (Total number of inpatient bed days added for a year (Attendance) 
xI00) / (Total Functional Beds x 365). 
32 As per data of BOR, there were 93 hospitals, but two Hospitals had not provided the 
information. 
29 Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-Ill: Healthcare Services 
• 23 per cent hospitals (21) had BOR of 80 per cent and more. Out of 
these hospitals, in 33 per cent hospitals (Seven), BOR exceeded 100 
percent. 
• 77 per cent hospitals (70) had BOR below 80 per cent. Out of these 
hospitals, 64 per cent hospitals (45) had BOR of less than 42 per cent.

3.1.4 Intensive Care Unit (ICU) 
Intensive Care Unit (ICU) is essential for critically ill patients requiring life­
saving medical aid and nursing care. As per IPHS, the number of ICU beds 
should be 5 to 10 per cent of the number of beds available in the hospital. 
i. Availability of ICU facility 
Audit examined availability of ICU services in the eight test-checked DHs and 
found that: 
• ICU facility was not available in two DHs (Salumber and Phalodi). 
• Three DHs (Baran, Sriganganagar and Jalore) had a shortage of more than 
50 per cent in ICU beds. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that five ICU beds have been 
constructed in DH Salumber and DH Phalodi had recently been upgraded and 
ICU beds will be provided in the upcoming financial year. It was further stated 
that ICU beds have been increased in DHs Baran, Sriganganagar and Jalore. 
ii. Average number of surgeries per surgeon 
Audit analysis of the information regarding number of surgeries and number 
of surgeons available in eight38 test-checked DHs revealed that the average 
number of surgeries per surgeon ranged between 20 (DH Jalore) and 390 (DH 
Sriganganagar) during 2021-22, as shown in Appendix 3.4.

did not give any reason for the decline in the vaccination percentage. It was 
further stated that as per availability of infrastructure, equipment, manpower 
and number of cold chain points are being gradually increased at CHCs/PHCs. 
iii. Benefits of Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY) to Below Poverty Line 
(BPL) families 
As per JSY guidelines, it is necessary that all women are encouraged to avail 
institutional care as institutional delivery is the primary strategy for promoting 
safe motherhood. However, in case a BPL pregnant woman, aged 19 years and 
above, prefers to deliver at home, she is entitled to cash assistance of ~  500 per 
delivery. 
Analysis of data available on PCTS portal for the period 2016-22 revealed that 
BPL mothers receiving JSY incentive for home deliveries was only 7 to 13 per 
cent of the overall home deliveries by BPL mothers during 2016-22. Details 
are given in Appendix 3. 6.

The State Government stated (January 2024) that out of 8,768 cases, 2,343 
beneficiaries have been paid JSY incentive. It was further stated that 5,581 
cases reported by error in PCTS portal and payment of rest 814 cases is in 
progress. Information regarding remaining 30 cases were not furnished by 
State Government.

3.2.2 Oxygen Services at Tertiary Care institutions 
As per National Medical Commission (NMC) (erstwhile MCI) Norms, 1999, 
all the Wards in Orthopaedic, Surgery and Paediatrics Departments should 
have wall-mounted suction lines, along with piped wall-mounted central 
oxygen lines, on all beds. Moreover, at least five beds in Ophthalmology 
Department should have the same arrangement. 
Scrutiny of records of two MCHs60 revealed that wall-mounted suction lines 
along with piped wall mounted central Oxygen lines were not available, as per 
details below: 
• In 379 beds (44 per cent) out of 853 beds of Orthopaedic, Surgery and 
Paediatrics Departments in both MCHs; 
• In any of the 30 beds in male and female Eye Wards of Ophthalmology 
Department at JLN Hospital, Ajmer. 
Details are given in Appendix 3.9. 
The State Government stated (June and July 2023) that wall mounted suction 
lines along with piped wall mounted central oxygen lines have been installed 
on each bed in all the departments of MB Government Hospital, Udaipur. It 
was further stated that efforts would be made to provide oxygen through 
pipeline in wards at JLN Hospital, Ajmer.

The State Government stated (January 2024) that if services were not provided 
within prescribed response time there is provision of imposing penalty. It was 
further stated that average response time for Urban, Rural and Desert areas 
were 7.78, 9.99 and 11.57 minutes respectively during April-November 2023. 
The reply is not tenable as the response time is crucial in ambulance services 
because it directly impacts the outcome of time-sensitive medical 
emergencies. Timely intervention can significantly improve a patient's chances 
of survival and recovery. Merely imposition of penalty does not serve the 
purpose.

Chapter-III : Healthcare Services 
3.2.6 Infection Control Management 
The National Health Mission Assessor's Guidebook requires that for cleaning 
and disinfection of patient care areas, standard practices are to be followed 
through maintenance of a checklist for hygiene and infection control in each 
hospital. Also, infection control policies are needed to be framed, practiced 
and monitored by the Hospital Infection Control Committee. 
Test-check of 60 GMis 71 revealed that checklist for Hygiene and Infection 
Control was available in only 15 out of 60 GMis (25 per cent). It was also 
found that Hospital Infection Control Committee (HICC) had been constituted 
in only 13 out of 36 GMis72 (36 per cent) as required. 
Details are given in Appendix 3.14(a) & (b). 
Improper management of garbage at DH Jalore (December 2021) and Rajkiya Mahi/a Chikitsalaya, Ajmer (February 2022) 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that periodically trainings are 
being imparted regarding infection control practices for nursing officers 
working in SDHs/DHs. 
Recommendation 3: The State Government should make efforts to provide 
adequate services such as Radiology, Pathology, Dietary, Ambulance and 
Mortuary Services as per IPHS in the GMis of the State.

HWCs.

Chapter-Ill : Healthcare Services 
3.3 Auxiliary Services 
3.3.1 Fire Fighting Services at Secondary and Tertiary Care Institutions 
The IPHS prescribes that building structure and internal structure of hospitals 
should be equipped with fire protection measures. Further, in every hospital, 
firefighting equipment such as fire extinguishers, sand buckets, etc. should be 
available and maintained for use in case of an incident. 
Joint Physical Verification conducted with departmental authorities in 14 
GMis 73 (April-July 2022) revealed that: 
• Fire No-Objection Certificate from competent authority was not obtained 
by 12 GMis (86 per cent); 
• In nine GMis (64 per cent), smoke detector, fire alarm were not available; 
• In ten GMis (71 per cent ), sand buckets were not available; 
• In seven GMis (50 per cent), fire hydrants were not installed and 
underground backup water for fire was not available; 
• In six GMis ( 43 per cent), plan for prevention of fire was not available; 
and 
• Nine GMis ( 64 per cent) did not conduct periodic training of staff and 
mock drills related to fire safety. 
Details are given in Appendix 3.15. 
The State Government stated (December 2023) that Fire No-Objection 
Certificate was obtained (November 2022) by Sir Padampat Institute of 
Neonatology and Pediatric Health, Jaipur. It was further stated that smoke 
detector and fire alarm have been installed and sand buckets are made 
available in Sir Padampat Institute of Neonatology and Pediatric Health, 
Jaipur. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that directions have been issued 
(December 2023) to submit proposals for installation firefighting system for 
inclusion of provisions in Project Implementation Plan. 
The fact remains that adequate firefighting services are not available in a 
significant number of Government Medical Institutions in the State. 
73 14 GMis: Eight DHs and Six MCHs. 
49 Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-III : Healthcare Services 
3.3.2 Patient Rights through Citizen Charter 
The National Health Mission Assessor's Guidebook and IPHS prescribe the 
requirement to display the Citizen's Charter at a suitable place in the hospital 
so that the patients are aware of their rights. 
Joint Physical Verification conducted with departmental authorities and 
information collected by Audit in the 92 test-checked GMis 74 (April-July 
2022 and May 2023) revealed that 57 GMis (62 per cent) had not displayed 
Citizen's Charter. Details are given in Appendix 3.16. 
The State Government stated (August 2023) that Citizen's Charter is available 
in Mahi/a Chikitsalya, Jaipur and Sir Padampat Institute of Neonatology and 
Pediatric Health, Jaipur. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that directions have been issued 
(December 2023) regarding display of Citizen's Charter. 
The fact remains that the norms regarding Citizen's Charter were not adhered 
to in a significant number of Government Medical Institutions due to which 
the public could not be made aware of their rights. 
Recommendation 4: The State Government should make efforts to address 
deficiencies in Fire Fighting Service in the GMls as per norms. 
74 92 GMis: 34 DHs, Four SDHs, Eight CHCs, 16 PHCs, 15 HSC, Nine HWCs and Six 
MCHs.

and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan"

Chapter-IV 
Availability of Drugs, Medicines, Equipment and Other 
Consumables 
Shortfall of essential drugs was noticed in the Government Medical Institutions 
of the State. The reasons contributing to this shortfall were the shortage of the 
drugs at the warehouses and inadequate supply of medicines to Government 
Medical Institutions by drug warehouses against their demand. Other reasons 
included delay in finalizing and renewal of Rate Contracts and delay in issuing 
Purchase Orders for supply of medicines. There were delays in the supply of 
ordered drugs by the vendors as well. 
Significant delays were noticed in the Quality Assurance process which 
contributed to the overall delays in the supply of drugs to the Government 
Medical Institutions. While the drug warehouses sent the drug samples for 
testing with delays, the testing laboratories also failed to furnish the quality test 
reports within the prescribed time limits. Instances of local purchase of drugs 
by the Government Medical Institutions without the batch test reports were also 
observed. 
There was significant shortfall in availability of essential equipment against 
IPHS at the test-checked District Hospitals, Sub-district Hospitals, Community 
Health Centres and Primary Health Centres. Scheduled preventive maintenance 
and calibration of equipment were not carried out as per the requirements. 
There were delays in the prescribed verification of equipment by the Medical 
Officer in charge of the Government Medical Institutions. Instances of non­
functional/idle equipment due to lack of trained manpower, non-availability of 
spare parts, etc. were also observed. 
4.1 Introduction 
The GoR set up the Rajasthan Medical Services Corporation Limited (RMSCL ), 
a State Public Sector Undertaking, on 4 May 2011 as a centralised procurement 
agency for procuring generic medicines, surgical and suture items and medical 
equipment at the most economical cost for all the Government Medical 
Institutions of DMH&FW and DME. The RMSCL has its own warehouses 
throughout the State for storing and distribution of drugs to medical institutions. 
The requirement of drugs, medicines, surgical and suture items and equipment 
are finalized by both Medical Directorates, i.e. Medical Education and Medical 
and Health Services, every year for centralised procurement by RMSCL. Need 
based procurement of drugs, surgical and suture items and equipment is made 
at State level by RMSCL. Thereafter, the drugs are delivered and stored at 
District Drug Warehouses 75 (DDWs) located in each district and Medical 
College Drug Warehouses 76 (MCDWs). The quality of drugs and surgical and 
75 34 DDWs in the State: One DDW each in 32 districts and two DDWs in Jaipur district. 
76 Six Medical College Drug Warehouses at Ajmer, Bikaner, Jaipur, Jodhpur, Kota and 
Udaipur in the State. 
51 Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-IV: Availability of Drugs, Medicines, Equipment and Other Consumables 
suture items is ensured through empanelled drug testing laboratories and after 
quality testing, these are issued to sub-stores of various Government Medical 
Institutions of the district as per their requirements. The drugs are finally 
distributed to the patients from Drug Distribution Centres (DDCs) of 
Government Medical Institutions. 
The activities ofRMSCL play a crucial role in the day-to-day functioning of the 
Government Medical Institutions. Two web applications (e-Aushadhi77 & 
e-Upkaran7~ for centralized inventory management are being used for 
monitoring of drugs and management and maintenance of equipment. Process 
flow charts of e-Aushadhi and e-Upkaran are given in Appendix 4.1. 
4.2 Availability of Drugs and Medicines 
4.2.1 Availability as reflected in e-Aushadhi System 
The RMSCL, as the central procurement body, centrally procures drugs and 
medicines included in the Essential Drug List (EDL) to be supplied to the 
Government Medical Institutions for free distribution to the beneficiaries 
under the Mukhyamantri Nishulk Dawa Yojana (MNDY 79). Number of 
drugs and consumables in the EDL (as on 11 March 2022) at the various 
medical units are as follows: 
• Primary Health Centres - 414 
• Community Health Centres - 635 
• District Hospitals - 844 
• Medical College Hospitals - 969 
The availability of these essential drugs and consumables at the various 
medical units at any point of time can be seen on the "Medicine Availability 
Tracker" 80 of e-Aushadhi web portal. Audit assessed the availability of these 
essential drugs at all the medical units of the State as on 11 March 2022 and 
observed that in 27 per cent of the PH Cs, the availability was less than 50 per 
cent of the listed drugs, as shown in Table 4.1 below:

The State Government stated (January 2024) that on the basis of demand and 
feedback received from the GMis, RMSCL regularly issued purchase orders 
for continuous supply of drugs and medicines at drugs warehouses. It was 
further stated that in case of delayed supply by supplier or non-availability of 
rate contract, GMis concerned after obtaining Non-Availability Certificate 
from drugs warehouses procure drugs through local purchase. 
The reply is not tenable as 50 per cent less availability of drugs and medicines 
were noticed in 27 per cent PHCs, 15 per cent CHCs and 43 per cent Medical 
College Hospitals and 75 per cent in Mother and Child Hospitals which shows 
that continuous supply of drugs were not ensured by RMSCL. 
Audit is of the opinion that the State Government may utilise the savings 
under provision for healthcare as mentioned in Paragraph 6.2 of the report to 
enhance the purchase of Drugs and Medicines with a view to address the 
shortages.

4.2.2 Assessment of availability of medicines based on prescriptions 
According to the directions of GoR (July 2013), the drugs which could not be 
made available to the patients in the GMis as per the patient voucher 
(prescription slip) were to be mandatorily entered in the e-Aushadhi system as 
'NA Hits' (Not Available drugs). 
For independent verification of the availability of drugs reflected in the 
e-Aushadhi system, Audit test-checked 153 prescription slips issued at various 
medical units during the five-year period 2017-22. In these slips, a total of 581 
drugs were prescribed to patients. In 66 prescription slips ( 43 per cent), the 
prescribed medicines were not available, out of which in 21 (14 per cent) cases, 
the non-availability of the prescribed drugs was not entered as 'NA Hits' in the 
e-Aushadhi system. 
The above fact raises questions over the veracity of the data regarding 
availability of medicines and has an impact on the demand projection of 
medicines as reflected in the e-Aushadhi system. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that in case a particular drug is 
not available then if any substitute is available, are made available to the 
patients by the GMis. It was further stated that in case where substitute is 
not available then after obtaining Non-Availability Certificate from the 
Drug Warehouse, drugs are being procured through local purchase by GMis. 
It was also stated that instruction have been issued regularly for ensure 
availability of drugs at GMis. 
The shortfall of medicines in Government Medical Institutions pre-dominantly 
affects poor people as they are mainly dependent on government hospitals for 
their healthcare needs and as such the State Government may take suitable steps 
to ensure adequate availability of medicines in Government Medical 
Institutions.

consumables to the GMis during the year. This has been shown in Table 4.2

To ensure uninterrupted supply of medicines, the procurement policy of

four months and a pipeline stock of two months.

The State Government (January 2024) attributed the reasons of non-availability 
of drugs and medicines to factors such as non-availability of rate contracts, 
limitations in placing additional order quantity to contracted firm (upto 
additional 50 per cent of contracted quantity as per Rajasthan Transparency 
Public Procurement Act), changing pattern of drug consumption according to 
seasons, etc. It was further stated that action is being taken as per norms against 
approved supplier for non-supply of drugs/surgical and sutures within the 
stipulated period. 
The reply is not tenable as 15 to 53 per cent of essential drugs and consumables 
were not available during June and July 2022 in the seven selected warehouses. 
Recommendation 5: The State Government may take necessary steps to ensure 
sufficient availability of essential drugs in the hospitals and drug warehouses.

Government needs to put in place a robust mechanism to monitor and ensure 
adequate supplies to GMis against their indents. 
4.3.4 Delay in finalising and renewal of Rate Contracts 
Procurement of medical supplies and drugs involve repeated purchases during 
the year, and therefore RMSCL had adopted a rate contract system. Suppliers 
for specific drugs to be selected through an open competitive bidding and rate 
contracts are signed with the Lowest Priced Technically Acceptable Bidder 
(Ll) . The duration of rate contract is two years and whenever the existing stock 
needs replenishment, supply orders are immediately placed on the selected 
vendor at the contracted price, without having to undergo the tendering process 
every time. However, any delay in procurement or finalizing of rate contracts 
results in consequent delays in the replenishment of stock. 
Out of a total 969 types of drugs and consumables to be procured by RMSCL, 
Audit observed that there were no rate contracts for five drugs for a period 
ranging between 37 days to 172 days (Appendix 4.2). 
Procurement Rules (RTPP Rules, 2013) stipulate that tendering should be 
completed within 50 days from the opening of the bids. 
It was found that in three out of these five drugs, the contract was finalized in 
112 days against the stipulated time of 50 days from the opening of technical 
bids. Details are in Appendix 4.3. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that delays in conclusion and 
renewal of rate contracts occur due to factors such as negotiation and counter­
offers to bidders and lack of bids in certain cases, etc. 
4.3.5 Delay in issue of purchase orders 
An efficient inventory management system which ensures timely supply and 
prevents stock-out, requires the maintenance of a buffer stock. The quantity of 
buffer stock for an item is equivalent to the estimated consumption of the item 
during the procurement lead time. 
RMSCL has adopted the practice of maintaining a buffer stock of four months 
based on the following: 
• Time for placing order - 10 days approx. ( after generation of Drug 
Report Card) 
• Time for delivery - 60 days maximum 
• Time taken for quality assurance/ testing - 30 days approx. 
• Time taken for replacement of quality rejected medicines - one month 
Audit noticed that there was no express provision in the procurement policy of 
RMSCL or Government orders specifying the point at which the purchase 
orders have to be placed for replenishment of stock. Therefore, the initiation of 
purchase orders was left to the discretion ofRMSCL and orders were placed in 
an ad-hoc manner.

4.3. 7 Supply of drugs and consumables with lesser shelf-life 
As per condition no. 13(4) of tender documents ofRMSCL, the remaining shelf 
life of the drugs at the time of delivery should not be less than three fourth of 
the labeled shelflife. Further, the conditions also mandate that the product with 
remaining shelf life less than the required shelf life should not be accepted. 
Audit analysis of data of e-Aushadhi revealed that out of 4,26,674 instances of 
drugs received at various Drugs warehouses of RMSCL during 2016-22, in 
3,301 instances, the remaining shelflife of the drugs at the time of delivery was 
less than three fourth of the labeled shelf life. Details of these 3,301 instances 
are provided in Table 4.7:

The State Government stated (Januaiy 2024) that in case of emergency

accept the supply of drugs with less than specified shelf life on case-to-case 
basis. 
The reply is not tenable as the acceptance of drugs with lesser shelf life not only 
give rise in the cases of expiry of drugs lying in the district drug warehouse or 
drugs stores of hospitals but also there is the possibility that the drugs may get 
expired by the time of distribution to the patients. 
Receipt of drugs having less than required remaining shelf life is not only in 
contravention of the conditions of the tender but also reflects a lack of 
monitoring at the level of Drug Warehouses. 
4.4 Quality Assurance 
4.4.1 Delay in sending samples/or quality check 
According to the Quality Control Policy and Procedures of RMSCL, supply of 
medicines received by the warehouses are to be quality tested before issuing 
them to the medical units. Samples are to be drawn by the warehouses from each 
batch of manufacture/supply and sent to RMSCL within two days of receipt of 
supplies. Until the quality test reports are received the drugs are quarantined by 
the warehouses. They are issued to the medical units only after the batch passes 
the quality test. 
Audit observed significant delays in sending of samples by the warehouses to 
RMSCL. Out of the 1,08,444 instances where samples were sent by warehouses 
to RMSCL, in 95 per cent cases the delay was upto one month, while in 5 per 
cent cases the delay was more than a month, as shown in Table 4.8 below: 
Table 4.8: Status of delay in sending samples for quality check 
No. of Cases 
Source: e-Aushadhi data provided by RMSCL. 
Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 60 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-IV: Availability of Drugs, Medicines, Equipment and Other Consumables 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that there was delay in sending the 
samples in some cases due to shortage of manpower and delay in transportation. 
It was further stated that the drug warehouses are being instructed again to 
adhere to the prescribed timeline. 
4.4.2 Delay in receipt of quality test reports from the laboratories 
As per the Quality Control Policy of RMSCL and conditions stipulated in the 
empanelment of testing laboratories, the laboratories are required to furnish the 
test reports within: 
(i) 10 days from the receipt of the sample, for non-sterile products like 
Tablets, Capsules, Pessaries, Ointments, Powder and Liquid 
Preparations. 
(ii) 21 days from the receipt of the sample, in case of I.V. Fluids and 
Injections. 
Audit observed delays in submission of test reports by the empaneled 
laboratories85. In analysis of total 86,965 cases during 2016-22. 
• In 17,967 out of 67,603 cases where the samples test reports were to be 
submitted within 10 days, and 
• In the remaining 19,362 cases, in 1,739 cases the reports were to be 
submitted within 21 days.

reasons, such as testing laboratory asking for manufacturer's Standard Testing 
Protocol (STP), Placebo and working standard for the purpose of testing in 
certain cases. The fact remains that there were inordinate delays in receipt of 
laboratory reports contributing to delays in supply of medicines to the GMis. 
Recommendation 7: The State Government may ensure timely testing of drug

Chapter-IV: Availability of Drugs, Medicines, Equipment and Other Consumables 
4.4.3 Local purchase of drugs and medicines 
Under Mukhyamantri Nishulk Dawa Yojana, GoR issued instructions (August 
2011) that GMis could utilize 10 per cent of the allotted annual budget on 
medicines for local purchase of medicines in case of non-availability or 
emergencies. 
(i) Excess local purchase of drugs and medicines 
Local purchase of drugs and medicines by the Government Medical Institutions 
is done through open tendering process as prescribed in Rajasthan Transparency 
in Public Procurement Act, 2012 and the firm with lowest rates being awarded 
the contract. 
Audit found that GMis made local purchase of drugs and medicines more than 
the prescribed limit of 10 per cent. Information provided by 22 test-checked 
GMis86 revealed that seven GMis made local purchases more than the 
prescribed limit of 10 per cent of their annual budget during the period 2016-22. 
Details are provided in Appendix 4.4. 
The State Government stated (December 2023) that due to non-supply as per 
demand and in time from RMSCL and to provide immediate treatment to 
patients, non-available medicines are purchased on contract basis through 
approved firms by JLN Hospital, Ajmer and Rajkiya Mahi/a Chikitsalya, Ajmer. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that instruction have been issued 
to GMis not to procure drugs and medicines at local level more than the 10 per 
cent of their annual budget. 
(ii) Local purchase of drugs and medicines without batch test reports 
As per the conditions of the standard bid document used by GMis for placing 
local purchase orders, test report from an accredited laboratory should be 
provided with the supply of medicines, in the absence of which the supply shall 
be rejected. Further, as per the Mukhyamantri Nishulk Dawa Yojana (MNDY) 
guidelines, the pharmacist should ensure that products of standard quality are 
purchased and distributed. 
In the test-checked GMis, it was found that during 2016-22, two87 out of eight 
DHs and one MCH out of six MCHs had purchased drugs locally without batch 
test reports88. 
86 22 GMis: Six MCHs, eight DHs and eight CHCs. 
87 Two DHs: DH Phalodi (Jodhpur) and DH Baran, One MCH: Shree Khemraj Katara 
Government Satellite Hospital, Udaipur. 
88 Requisite batch testing reports from approved drug testing laboratory regarding quality of 
drugs being supplied was to be furnished by supplier at the time of supply of medicines. 
Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 62 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-IV: Availability of Drugs, Medicines, Equipment and Other Consumables 
Audit examination also revealed that two DHs89 had failed to enter the details 
of the locally procured drugs and medicines in e-Aushadhi . 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that directions have been issued to 
all CMHOs and PMOs for entering details of local purchase of drugs in 
e-Aushadhi system. Reply regarding drugs were purchased locally without batch 
test report were awaited (March 2024). 
Recommendation 8: The State Government may ensure issue of drugs to GMls 
only after receipt of quality test reports. 
4.5 Equipment 
4.5.1 Availability of equipment as per IPHS Norms 
Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) stipulates the number of essential 
equipment under different categories based on the type/level of the hospitals 
(number of beds). Audit examination revealed that the required equipment in 
various categories were not available in the test-checked units. 
(i) Availability of equipment in DHs 
According to IPHS, 312 types of equipment required in DH having 100 to 200 
beds, 332 types of equipment required in DH having 201 to 300 beds and 339 
types of equipment required in DH having 301 and above belonging to 16 
selected essential categories are required at DH level. 
Audit observed (April-July 2022) that none of the sampled eight DHs were fully 
equipped in the selected 16 categories of essential equipment such as Ear Nose 
Throat Equipment, Eye Equipment, Dental Equipment, Laboratory Equipment, 
Endoscopy Equipment and Operation Theatre Equipment as per IPHS norms. It 
was observed that in the eight sampled DHs, against the requirement of 2,610 
equipment90 prescribed by IPHS for various services, only 1,400 equipment91 
were available. Thus, there was a shortfall of 1,210 ( 46 per cent) equipment in 
the sampled DHs as shown in Appendix 4.5. 
(ii) Availability of equipment in SDHs/SHs 
According to IPHS, 189 types92 and 192 types 93 of equipment belonging to 14 
essential categories are required at SDHs. 
Audit observed (April-July 2022) in the four94 sampled SDHs/SHs, against the 
765 equipment 95 required for various services under 14 essential categories, 
89 DH Phalodi (Jodhpur) and DH Kotputli (Jaipur). 
90 Total requirement of equipment in DHs as per IPHS = 312+312+312+332+ 
332+ 332+ 339+ 339=2,6 l 0.

(iii) Availability of equipment in CH Cs 
As per the IPHS for CHC, 269 types of equipment under 15 different categories 
are deemed essential for CHCs. 
Audit observed (April-July 2022) that in the eight test-checked CHCs, a total of 
1,000 equipment 97 (46.47 per cent) were available against the requirement of 
2,152 equipment 98 as per IPHS. Thus, there was a shortfall of 1,152 (53.53 per 
cent) equipment in the sampled CHCs as shown in Appendix 4. 7. 
(iv) Availability of Equipment in PHCs 
IPHS prescribes 94 equipment under five essential categories for PHCs. 
Audit observed (April-July 2022) that in 16 test-checked PHCs, a total of 1,057 
equipment99 (five categories) were available against the total requirement of 
1,504 equipment100 prescribed by IPHS, reflecting an overall shortfall of 29.72 
per cent essential equipment in the test-checked PHCs. Details are shown in 
Appendix 4.8. 
The State Government (September 2023) stated that equipment has been 
supplied on the basis of demand received from National Health 
Mission/Directorate. 
The reply of Government is not tenable as all the equipment are of essential 
categories and should be made available to the GMis irrespective of their 
demands. 
Lack of essential equipment may result in inadequate diagnosis and 
investigation of patients thereby affecting satisfactory delivery of healthcare 
services.

Medical equipments are an important aspect of healthcare as they help diagnose, 
monitor and treat a wide ranges of medical conditions . Equipment's like Dental 
X-ray machine, Dental X-ray chair are essential for diagnostic and treatment of 
Dental diseases. Further Fetal Doppler is important for monitoring fetus health.

machine are vital for conducting surgeries.

Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 68 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-V 
Healthcare Infrastructure 
There was shortage of healthcare infrastructure across the State with the 
shortage being most acute in the case of primary healthcare in the tribal and 
plain areas. The State Government did not have a plan to prioritise areas with 
shortfall for setting up of healthcare infrastructure. 
There was uneven distribution of bed capacities among the District/Sub-district 
hospitals. A large number of government medical institutions lacked basic 
amenities, such as clean drinking water facility, separate toilets for males and 
females, etc. 
The Primary and Secondary Care institutions were deficient in several key 
requirements, such as availability of Major Operation Theatre, Preparation 
Room, Pre-operative Rooms. 
The Tertiary Care institutions were deficient in various aspects related to 
Wards, such as Clinical Demonstration Rooms, Examination and Treatment 
Rooms, Resident Doctors and Student Duty Rooms, etc. They were also deficient 
in facilities required in Operation Theatres, such as Post-operative Recovery 
Rooms. 
5.1 Availability of Healthcare Infrastructure in the State 
Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) require that one Health Sub-Centre 
(HSC) should be established for every 5,000 population in plain areas and for 
every 3,000 population in hilly/tribal/desert areas. A Primary Health Centre 
(PHC) covers a population of20,000 in hilly, tribal and desert areas and 30,000 
population in plain areas, whereas each Community Health Centre (CHC) 
generally includes four PHCs and caters to approximately 80,000 population in 
tribal/hilly/desert areas and 1,20,000 population in plain areas.

iii. Plan/or establishment of new PHCs and HSCs 
In the budget announcement for the year 2019-20, the State Government 
proposed the establishment of 200 new HSCs and 100 new PH Cs in the State. 
Out of these, approval for 118 new HSCs (belonging to 24 districts 112) were 
given, however, funds were released for 73 HSCs only (till May 2023). 
Similarly, 37 PHCs (in 19 districts 113) were proposed and funds were released 
for 26 PHCs (till May 2023). 
Details are given in Appendix 5.1. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that healthcare facilities are 
opened and upgraded in the State as per norms determined by Gol. It was further 
stated that the opening of healthcare facilities is considered according to the 
availability of financial resources, requirement and after approval of competent 
authority in districts having shortage of HSCs/PHCs/CHCs. It was also stated 
that District Hospital in Bharatpur, Jhalawar and Pali will be opened and in 
Churu and Kota district, SRJ Hospital, Ratangarh (Churu) and Government 
District Hospital, Kota are designated as DH. 
This indicates that State Government did not have a concrete plan to address the 
shortage and prioritise areas with shortfall for setting up of healthcare 
infrastructure. 
Recommendation 10: The State Government may ensure availability of 
healthcare institutions as per Indian Public Health Standards in the State and 
their equitable distribution to make healthcare accessible for all.

Examination of availability of beds in the District Hospitals 114 of the State 
revealed that out of 34 test-checked DHs, in ten DHs (Alwar, Barmer, Bhilwara, 
Bikaner, Jaipur, Jalore, Jodhpur, Nagaur, Sikar and Udaipur), the deficiency of

(ii) Availability of Beds against Norms in test-checked CHCs and PHCs 
IPHS prescribed minimum availability of four-six and 30 beds for a Primary 
Health Centre (PHC) and Community Health Centre (CHC) respectively. 
Examination of availability of beds in the test-checked eight CHCs and 16 PHCs 
revealed that there was a shortage of beds in two PHCs (Baldara and Bhasawata 
Kalan) and in four CHCs (Jobner, Singhana, Asop and Sarada). Details are 
given in Appendix 5.3. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that sanction for increase in bed 
capacity has been issued on case-to-case basis and as per utility of beds 
requirement at competent level. 
(iii) Availability of basic amenities in healthcare facilities 
IPHS require that Primary Health Centres and Health Sub-Centres should have 
their own buildings. If that is not possible, premises with adequate space should 
be rented in a central location with easy access to the population. The PHC/HSC 
should have facilities for electricity, all weather road, communication and 
separate toilets for males and females in addition to adequate availability of 
potable water. 
As per the information provided by the State Government, out of 4,640 GMis 
in eight test-checked districts, 1,021 GMis 115 (22 per cent) did not comply with 
the above norms. Further, the following deficiencies were also brought out: 
• Buildings of 21 per cent GMis were in dilapidated condition and required 
repairs, 
• 50 per cent GMis had common toilets for males and females, 
• 812 GMis (17 per cent), including one CHC, 15 PHCs and 796 HSCs, had 
no electricity connection and 22 per cent GMis 116 had no water connection. 
Details are given in Appendix 5.4. 
Non-availability of electricity in the GMis makes them incapable of proper 
storage of vaccines and many medicines, thus depriving the patients of the same. 
Audit noticed that the utilisation under the 'Community Sanitary Complexes' 
component of Swachh Bharat Mission-Gramin in the State was 36 per cent

by the Department.

77 Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure

Performance Audit of "Public Health Infrastructure 78 
and Management of Health Services in Rajasthan" 

Chapter-VI 
Financial Management 
Audit noticed that State specific Health Policy has yet not been framed in the 
State. Audit also noticed persistent savings and inadequate utilisation of 
available funds in the Healthcare sector, which is indicative of ineffective 
budgetary controls in the departments. Total Health Expenditure and 
Government Health Expenditure increased in absolute terms during 2016-17 to 
2019-20 in the State. 
This chapter comments on the Policy planning, provision, allocation, availability 
and expenditure of funds towards Healthcare in Rajasthan. 
The financing to the State is based on the State's Programme Implementation 
Plan (SPIP). The SPIP is a document to be prepared by the States annually 
which helps them in identifying and quantifying their targets required for 
programme implementation for the proposed year. 
6.1 Non formulation of State Health Policy 
Public health is a State subject and States are expected to frame their own 
policies on the lines of National Health Policy (NHP) to guide the development 
of health sector in each State. Audit observed that the GoR had not framed a 
State specific Health Policy till date (May 2023). 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that State Health Policy has not 
been framed, but State is making efforts to comply National Health Policy as 
far as possible. 
6.2 Financial Management 
Adequate and timely availability of funds is a fundamental pre-requisite for the 
delivery of quality and efficient healthcare services. Funds to the GMis are 
made available through the State budget. Financial assistance under the NHM 
is also received from the Gol with corresponding share of the State Government. 
Apart from the State funding, expenditure is also incurred through the regular 
budget heads of DMH&FW and DME. 
The year-wise budget estimates and actual expenditure during 2016-17 to 2021-22 
in DMH&FW and DME are shown in Table 6.1 below:

As seen from Table 6.3, Total Health Expenditure (THE) and Government 
Health Expenditure (GHE) increased in absolute terms during 2016-17 to 2019-20 
in State. 
The percentage of GHE to THE also increased from 33.00 per cent (2016-17) 
to 42.40 per cent (2019-20). 
However, THE as a percentage of GSDP decreased from 3 .4 in 2016-17 to 3 .1 
in 2019-20 while GHE as a percentage of GSDP increased from 1.1 (2016-17) 
to 1.3 (2019-20) in Rajasthan. 
Out-of-pocket expenditure is directly made by a household at the point of 
receiving Healthcare and indicates the extent of financial protection available 
to households towards healthcare payments. As seen from Table 6.3, though 
the percentage of out-of-pocket expenditure to THE decreased in Rajasthan 
during the period 2016-20, it constituted 47.40 per cent of THE during 2019-20, 
indicating low financial protection available to households towards 
healthcare payments in the State. 
The State Government stated (January 2024) that budget proposals were 
submitted to Budget Finalization Committee. It was further stated that the 
decision of fund allocation in all heads was taken and finalized after discussion 
held with the finance department. However, the point remains same that the 
State Government should take necessary steps to increase the budgetary 
allocation for healthcare sector. 
Recommendation 13: The State Government may take necessary steps to 
increase the budgetary allocation for Healthcare sector both as a percentage 
of State Budget as well as Gross State Domestic Product, in line with the 
National Health Policy.

The Health and Wellness Centres established under Ayushman Bharat scheme
